</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background:</h3>
<p>Exposure to per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) is ubiquitous and has been associated with an increased risk of several cardiometabolic diseases. However, the metabolic pathways linking PFAS exposure and human disease are unclear.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Objective:</h3>
<p>We examined associations of PFAS mixtures with alterations in metabolic pathways in independent cohorts of adolescents and young adults.</p></section><section id="sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods:</h3>
<p>Three hundred twelve overweight/obese adolescents from the Study of Latino Adolescents at Risk (SOLAR) and 137 young adults from the Southern California Children’s Health Study (CHS) were included in the analysis. Plasma PFAS and the metabolome were determined using liquid-chromatography/high-resolution mass spectrometry. A metabolome-wide association study was performed on log-transformed metabolites using Bayesian regression with a g-prior specification and g-computation for modeling exposure mixtures to estimate the impact of exposure to a mixture of six ubiquitous PFAS (PFOS, PFHxS, PFHpS, PFOA, PFNA, and PFDA). Pathway enrichment analysis was performed using Mummichog and Gene Set Enrichment Analysis. Significance across cohorts was determined using weighted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i1" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic z"><mi>Z</mi></math></span>-tests.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Results:</h3>
<p>In the SOLAR and CHS cohorts, PFAS exposure was associated with alterations in tyrosine metabolism (meta-analysis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i2" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 0.00002"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.00002</mn></mrow></math></span>) and <em>de novo</em> fatty acid biosynthesis (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i3" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 0.03"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.03</mn></mrow></math></span>), among others. For example, when increasing all PFAS in the mixture from low (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i4" display="inline" alttext="approximately 30"><mrow><mo>∼</mo><mn>30</mn></mrow></math></span>th percentile) to high (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i5" display="inline" alttext="approximately 70"><mrow><mo>∼</mo><mn>70</mn></mrow></math></span>th percentile), thyroxine (T4), a thyroid hormone related to tyrosine metabolism, increased by 0.72 standard deviations (SDs; equivalent to a standardized mean difference) in the SOLAR cohort (95% Bayesian credible interval (BCI): 0.00, 1.20) and 1.60 SD in the CHS cohort (95% BCI: 0.39, 2.80). Similarly, when going from low to high PFAS exposure, arachidonic acid increased by 0.81 SD in the SOLAR cohort (95% BCI: 0.37, 1.30) and 0.67 SD in the CHS cohort (95% BCI: 0.00, 1.50). In general, no individual PFAS appeared to drive the observed associations.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion:</h3>
<p>Exposure to PFAS is associated with alterations in amino acid metabolism and lipid metabolism in adolescents and young adults. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11372" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11372</a></p></section></section><section id="s1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) make up a prevalent class of persistent organic pollutants with endocrine and metabolism disrupting properties.<sup><a href="#c1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c1">1</a>,<a href="#c2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c2">2</a></sup> PFAS are used in a broad range of industrial and consumer products, such as firefighting foams, nonstick pans, waterproof clothing, food packaging, and even cosmetic products, including lipstick.<sup><a href="#c3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c3">3</a>,<a href="#c4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c4">4</a></sup> Because of their widespread use and resistance to chemical degradation, PFAS can be found in drinking water and food sources across the world. In the United States, an estimated <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i6" display="inline" alttext="200 million"><mrow><mn>200</mn><mtext> million</mtext></mrow></math></span> people have drinking water with perfluorooctane sulfonic acid (PFOS) or perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) levels <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i7" display="inline" alttext="greater than 1 nanogram per liter"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>1</mn><mtext> ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>,<sup><a href="#c5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c5">5</a></sup> which is considerably higher than the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2022 safe drinking water health advisory levels of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i8" display="inline" alttext="4 times 10 begin superscript negative 6 end superscript nanogram per liter"><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></mrow></msup><mtext>ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> for PFOA and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i9" display="inline" alttext="2 times 10 begin superscript negative 6 end superscript nanogram per liter"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></mrow></msup><mtext>ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> for PFOS.<sup><a href="#c6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c6">6</a>,<a href="#c7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c7">7</a></sup> Because of their chemical properties, some PFAS also bioaccumulate in human tissues, with estimated half-lives of up to 25 y.<sup><a href="#c8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c8">8</a></sup> Consequently, several legacy PFAS, including PFOS, PFOA, perfluorohexanesulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluoroheptanesulfonic acid (PFHpS), perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA), and perfluorodecanoic acid (PFDA) are detectible in the blood of nearly all humans.<sup><a href="#c9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c9">9</a>,<a href="#c10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c10">10</a></sup> Therefore, it is of utmost importance to determine how exposure to PFAS impacts human health to inform public health policy and reduce exposure levels for harmful PFAS.</p>
<p>Accumulating evidence suggests that PFAS exposure is associated with an increased risk of metabolic disorders.<sup><a href="#c11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c11">11</a></sup> PFAS exposure during sensitive periods of development, such as childhood or adolescence, is of particular concern because this is an important developmental stage for cellular differentiation and development of metabolic tissues.<sup><a href="#c12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c12">12</a>–<a href="#c15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c15">15</a></sup> Longitudinal studies have found that PFAS exposure during childhood is associated with the development of dysregulated glucose metabolism and insulin resistance,<sup><a href="#c16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c16">16</a>,<a href="#c17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c17">17</a></sup> dyslipidemia,<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a></sup> and adiposity.<sup><a href="#c19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c19">19</a>,<a href="#c20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c20">20</a></sup> Similar associations have been observed in adults, suggesting that these associations persist into adulthood.<sup><a href="#c21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c21">21</a>–<a href="#c23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c23">23</a></sup> However, the mechanisms linking PFAS exposure and metabolic disorders in humans remain unclear, especially in children.</p>
<p>Studies examining the associations of PFAS with alterations in targeted biomarkers have been used to examine the potential mechanisms linking PFAS with metabolic disorders. These targeted biomarker studies have focused primarily on metabolites known to be associated with a specific metabolic disease, with a particular focus on metabolites related to lipid metabolism. For example, cross-sectional and longitudinal human studies have consistently shown that PFAS exposure increases serum total and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol in both children and adults.<sup><a href="#c11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c11">11</a></sup> Dyslipidemia is a hallmark of metabolic syndrome and can increase the risk for metabolic and cardiovascular disease,<sup><a href="#c24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c24">24</a></sup> suggesting that PFAS-associated alterations in lipids may mediate the relationship between PFAS exposure and risk of metabolic disorders. However, using a targeted approach to study the impact of PFAS exposure on alterations in metabolites does not provide information about how PFAS alter metabolic networks, which is important for understanding the mechanisms linking PFAS exposure with metabolic disorders.</p>
<p>Untargeted metabolomics approaches can quantify thousands of metabolites, which can provide insight on the metabolic perturbations of PFAS exposure. Although previous studies have used metabolomics to examine the associations of PFAS exposure and alterations in metabolic pathways, important gaps in the literature remain.<sup><a href="#c25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c25">25</a></sup>
<em>In vivo</em> and <em>in vitro</em> models have shown that exposure to individual PFAS alters fatty acid metabolism, including in human cell lines,<sup><a href="#c26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c26">26</a></sup> rodents,<sup><a href="#c27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c27">27</a>–<a href="#c29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c29">29</a></sup> and zebrafish.<sup><a href="#c30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c30">30</a></sup> In humans, studies using both targeted and untargeted metabolomics methods have reported similar associations between exposure to individual PFAS, including PFOS, PFHxS, and PFOA, and fatty acid metabolism.<sup><a href="#c31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c31">31</a>–<a href="#c40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c40">40</a></sup> Other studies have reported associations between PFAS exposure and metabolic pathways such as amino acid<sup><a href="#c31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c31">31</a>,<a href="#c34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c34">34</a>,<a href="#c37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c37">37</a>,<a href="#c40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c40">40</a>,<a href="#c41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c41">41</a></sup> or bile acid metabolism,<sup><a href="#c39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c39">39</a>,<a href="#c42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c42">42</a>,<a href="#c43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c43">43</a></sup> both of which are important pathways that can contribute to the pathogenesis of diseases, such as type 2 diabetes and cancer.<sup><a href="#c44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c44">44</a>–<a href="#c47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c47">47</a></sup> Together, these human studies present a myriad of sometimes conflicting results. Although differences in study populations and the use of targeted vs. untargeted metabolomic methods may account for some of the differences across studies, the majority of existing studies have examined the metabolic perturbations of PFAS congeners individually, using single exposure models. In reality, individuals are exposed to a complex mixture of potentially correlated PFAS compounds that may have synergistic effects on human metabolism.<sup><a href="#c48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c48">48</a></sup> Previous studies examining the impact of PFAS mixtures on the metabolome have relied primarily on reducing the dimensionality of the correlated PFAS exposures prior to analysis using either principal components or by calculating the sum of PFAS congeners.<sup><a href="#c25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c25">25</a></sup> However, it is difficult to draw conclusions about the effects of different components of the PFAS mixture from these studies owing to difficulties in interpreting principal components or summed exposure variables. Despite the analytical difficulties, examining the effects of PFAS mixtures on metabolic pathways is key to fully understand the consequences of exposure to these chemicals. Further, understanding how PFAS mixtures impact human metabolism can help to put previous studies on individual PFAS congeners in greater context.</p>
<p>In the present study, we aimed to examine the impact of PFAS mixtures on metabolic pathways in independent cohorts of children and young adults. By using two cohorts, we aimed to identify associations between PFAS mixtures and metabolic pathways that were consistent across cohorts despite varied background characteristics. We used high-resolution mass spectrometry (HRMS)–based untargeted metabolomics coupled with a Bayesian hierarchical regression with a g-prior specification and g-computation for modeling exposure mixtures to estimate the joint effects of PFAS mixtures on metabolic pathways.</p></section><section id="s2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<section id="s2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Study Populations</h3>
<section id="s2.1.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Study of Latino Adolescents at Risk.</h4>
<p>This study used data from the Study of Latino Adolescents at Risk (SOLAR). As described previously,<sup><a href="#c16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c16">16</a>,<a href="#c49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c49">49</a>–<a href="#c51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c51">51</a></sup> the SOLAR cohort included 328 overweight/obese children recruited in two waves between 2001 and 2012. Participants completed yearly clinical visits at the General Clinical Research Center of the Clinical Trials Unit at the University of Southern California. At baseline, participants were between 8 and 13 years of age and were overweight or obese based on sex and age-specific body mass index (BMI) percentile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i10" display="inline" alttext="greater than 85 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">85</mi><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>. Participants were included in the study if they were Hispanic or Latino based on all parents and grandparents self-reporting as Hispanic or Latino and if they had a direct family history of type 2 diabetes. Participants were excluded from the study if they had type 1 or type 2 diabetes or if they were on medications known to influence glucose or insulin metabolism. For the present analysis, participants were included if they completed a 2-h oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) during their first or second visit, resulting in a total sample size of 312 participants. The institutional review board at the University of Southern California provided ethics approval for this study, and participants and their guardians provided written informed assent/consent prior to participation.</p></section><section id="s2.1.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Southern California Children’s Health Study.</h4>
<p>To examine the generalizability of findings from the SOLAR cohort to young adults, we analyzed data from the Metabolic and Asthma Incidence Research (Meta-AIR) study.<sup><a href="#c52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c52">52</a></sup> Meta-AIR was a study including 172 young adults between the ages of 17 and 23 years of age who were part of the Southern California Children’s Health Study (CHS).<sup><a href="#c53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c53">53</a></sup> Meta-AIR participants were recruited for a single clinical visit between 2014 and 2018. Clinical visits occurred at the Clinical Trials Unit or the Diabetes and Obesity Research institute at the University of Southern California, during which participants performed a 2-h OGTT and completed detailed questionnaires. CHS participants were selected for inclusion in the Meta-AIR study if they had overweight or obesity between 14 and 15 years of age. Overweight and obesity were based on the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force guidelines, defined as having a sex- and age-specific BMI percentile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i11" display="inline" alttext="greater than 85 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">85</mi><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>.<sup><a href="#c54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c54">54</a></sup> Participants were excluded if they had a diagnosis of diabetes mellitus or if they took any medications that could influence glucose metabolism or insulin secretion. Meta-AIR participants were included in the present study if they provided consent for future use of biospecimens, resulting in a total sample size of 137. The University of Southern California institutional review board provided ethics approval for this study, and participants (and their guardians if participants were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i12" display="inline" alttext="less than 18 years"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>18</mn><mtext> years</mtext></mrow></math></span> of age) provided written informed assent/consent prior to participation.</p></section></section><section id="s2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Covariates</h3>
<p>Detailed information on the measurement of covariates in the SOLAR and CHS cohorts has been provided previously.<sup><a href="#c16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c16">16</a>,<a href="#c49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c49">49</a>–<a href="#c53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c53">53</a></sup> Height (in meters) and weight (in kilograms) were measured at each visit in both cohorts and were used to calculate BMI as kilograms per meter squared. In both cohorts, participants completed questionnaires related to sociodemographics and individual and familial health history. In the SOLAR cohort, socioeconomic status (SES) was characterized using a modified version of the Hollingshead Four-Factor Index,<sup><a href="#c55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c55">55</a></sup> as described previously.<sup><a href="#c49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c49">49</a></sup> The Hollingshead Four-Factor Index was modified to provide a single score for children and takes into account the education, occupation, and marital status of parents or primary caregivers.<sup><a href="#c49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c49">49</a></sup> In the CHS cohort, parental education was used to assess SES.<sup><a href="#c52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c52">52</a></sup> For the primary analysis, SES scores were grouped into quantiles and analyzed numerically, with values ranging from 1 to 4, with 1 representing the lowest and 4 representing the highest quantile of SES. In the SOLAR cohort, SES was unavailable for 11% (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i13" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 35"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>35</mn></mrow></math></span>) of individuals. In these participants, we imputed the population median SES score; median imputation has been shown to perform similarly to more complex imputation methods when the proportion of missing values is relatively low.<sup><a href="#c56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c56">56</a></sup> Further, as described previously, participants with missing SES data did not differ in metabolic or physical attributes, including sex (tested using a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i14" display="inline" alttext="lowercase chi square"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">χ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> test; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i15" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 0.35"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.35</mn></mrow></math></span>), age (tested using an independent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i16" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic t"><mi>t</mi></math></span>-test; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i17" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 0.88"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.88</mn></mrow></math></span>), or BMI (tested using an independent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i18" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic t"><mi>t</mi></math></span>-test; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i19" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 0.94"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.94</mn></mrow></math></span>).<sup><a href="#c16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c16">16</a>,<a href="#c51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c51">51</a></sup></p>
<p>In the SOLAR cohort, Tanner stages were determined by a physician during a physical exam to assess sexual maturity.<sup><a href="#c57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c57">57</a>,<a href="#c58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c58">58</a></sup> Tanner stages range from 1 to 5 and were categorized as follows: stage 1: prepuberty, which is the developmental stage before secondary sex characteristics begin to develop; stages 2–4: puberty, which is the developmental stage when secondary sex characteristics begin developing and menarche occurs for most females; stage 5: postpuberty, the developmental stage where secondary sex characteristics, including pubic hair and secondary sex organs, reach maturity.</p>
<p>Covariates included in models were selected using directed acyclic graphs (DAGs; Figure S1) and included age (in years), sex (male/female, coded numerically as 0/1), BMI (in kilograms per meter squared), and SES (in quantiles, coded numerically as 1–4). In the SOLAR cohort, Tanner stage (Tanner stages 1–5, coded numerically as 1–5) and study wave (Wave 1/Wave 2, coded numerically as 0/1) were also included as covariates. No covariates for statistical models contained missing data, except SES in the SOLAR cohort (as described above).</p></section><section id="s2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Plasma PFAS</h3>
<p>Six common PFAS (PFOS, PFHxS, PFHpS, PFOA, PFNA, and PFDA) were quantified in plasma samples collected at the 2-h OGTT time point. PFAS levels were quantified in batches of 70 study samples via liquid chromatography (LC) coupled to HRMS (LC-HRMS). The specific LC-HRMS protocol used to measure plasmas PFAS for this study has been described previously.<sup><a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup> Briefly, plasma samples were prepared by combining <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i20" display="inline" alttext="40 microliters"><mrow><mn>40</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of plasma with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i21" display="inline" alttext="2.5 microliters"><mrow><mn>2.5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of an internal standard. The internal standard contained thirty <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i22" display="inline" alttext="begin superscript 13 end superscript uppercase c"><mrow><mmultiscripts><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><none></none><mrow><mn>13</mn></mrow></mmultiscripts></mrow></math></span>-labeled PFAS to obtain a final <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i23" display="inline" alttext="5 nanograms per milliliter"><mrow><mn>5</mn><mtext> ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span> concentration. Proteins were then precipitated by adding <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i24" display="inline" alttext="80 microliters"><mrow><mn>80</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of cold acetonitrile, then vortexing and centrifuging at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i25" display="inline" alttext="18,000 times centrifugal force"><mrow><mn>18,000</mn><mo>×</mo><mi>g</mi></mrow></math></span> for 15 min. LC-MS grade water was added to the resulting supernatant to achieve a dilution ratio of 2:1. PFAS analysis was completed by reverse phase (RP) chromatography with negative electrospray ionization, performed with a Thermo Scientific Vanquish Flex ultra-high-performance LC system with binary pump attached to a Thermo Scientific Q Exactive HF-X Orbitrap mass spectrometry system (Thermo Fisher Scientific).</p>
<p>After analysis of samples from both the SOLAR and CHS cohorts, peaks for PFAS and their corresponding <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i26" display="inline" alttext="begin superscript 13 end superscript uppercase c"><mrow><mmultiscripts><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mprescripts></mprescripts><none></none><mrow><mn>13</mn></mrow></mmultiscripts></mrow></math></span>-labeled PFAS were identified by matching mass <em>m/z</em> with a tolerance of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i27" display="inline" alttext="5 parts per million"><mrow><mn>5</mn><mtext> ppm</mtext></mrow></math></span>, then extracted and integrated using TraceFinder 5.1 (Thermo Fisher Scientific). Quantification of PFAS was performed by dividing the peak for a given PFAS with its corresponding internal standard and comparing this value to a calibration curve with 6 points created using charcoal-stripped plasma. Replicate measures of National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) 1957 and NIST 1958 standard reference material were performed for every batch of 70 samples. Each batch also included instrumental and method blanks. The coefficient of variation for major PFAS was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i28" display="inline" alttext="less than 15 percent"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>15</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>, whereas the analyte recovery was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i29" display="inline" alttext="greater than 90 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">90</mi><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>. The accuracy of the method was assessed by comparing with NIST standard reference materials. Method accuracy was also assessed by participating in the Center de toxicologie du Québec’s Arctic Monitoring and Assessment Program Ring Test for Persistent Organic Pollutants in Human Serum. The limits of detection (LODs) were as follows: PFOS: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i30" display="inline" alttext="0.43 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.43</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>; PFHxS: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i31" display="inline" alttext="0.01 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.01</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>; PFHpS: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i32" display="inline" alttext="0.05 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.05</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>; PFOA: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i33" display="inline" alttext="0.01 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.01</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>; PFNA: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i34" display="inline" alttext="0.01 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.01</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>; and PFDA: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i35" display="inline" alttext="0.01 micrograms per liter"><mrow><mn>0.01</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span>. PFDA was the only PFAS included in the present study that had values below the LOD; these values were imputed as LOD divided by the square root of 2.</p>
<p>To contextualize exposure levels in the SOLAR and CHS cohorts, we compared the geometric mean and 95% confidence interval of PFAS concentrations to appropriate period- and age-matched PFAS concentrations from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES).<sup><a href="#c60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c60">60</a></sup> For the SOLAR cohort, PFAS concentrations were compared with those in young persons 12–19 years of age from the NHANES survey years 2007–2008, given that this was near the middle of the study period for this cohort. For the CHS cohort, PFAS concentrations were compared with those in young persons 12–19 years of age from the NHANES survey years 2017–2018, which overlapped in time with the CHS cohort and was the only survey year in which PFHpS concentrations were reported.</p></section><section id="s2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Untargeted Plasma Metabolomics</h3>
<p>Untargeted plasma metabolomics were measured using samples collected at the 2-h OGTT time point. Previous studies have shown that metabolite concentrations following a glucose challenge are potentially more informative when examining alterations in metabolic pathways because the glucose challenge is a physiological stressor that requires metabolic flexibility.<sup><a href="#c61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c61">61</a>,<a href="#c62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c62">62</a></sup> Measuring untargeted metabolomics was performed using established methods,<sup><a href="#c63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c63">63</a></sup> as described previously.<sup><a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup> Briefly, LC-HRMS was performed with RP and hydrophilic interaction LC (HILIC) by employing a dual polarity/dual column approach. Analyses were performed with a Thermo Fisher Vanquish Duo LC system with dual pumps and columns with independent flow paths connected to a Thermo Fisher Q Exactive HF-X Orbitrap MS system. Samples were analyzed in RP and HILIC separately to allow for the analysis of all four LC-HRMS modes.</p>
<p>Before analysis, samples for RP were prepared using the methods described above in the section “Plasma PFAS”.<sup><a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup> Samples for HILIC were prepared using the methods described above, with minor alterations in the volumes as described previously. Specifically, for HILIC mode, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i36" display="inline" alttext="90 microliters"><mrow><mn>90</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of acetonitrile was added to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i37" display="inline" alttext="30 microliters"><mrow><mn>30</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of plasma before processing was continued as described above. For HILIC mode, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i38" display="inline" alttext="30 microliters"><mrow><mn>30</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> of the processed supernatant was added to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i39" display="inline" alttext="90 microliters"><mrow><mn>90</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> 1:1 (vol/vol) water/acetonitrile. As described previously, samples were analyzed with both positive and negative ionization by optimizing mobile phases.<sup><a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup> Separation for RP was performed using <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i40" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 18 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>18</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> (TARGA <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i41" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 18 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>18</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i42" display="inline" alttext="5 micrometers"><mrow><mn>5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i43" display="inline" alttext="50 times 2.1 millimeters"><mrow><mn>50</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>2.1</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mm</mi></mrow></math></span>; Higgins Analytical), whereas separation for HILIC was performed using a SeQuant ZIC-HILIC column (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i44" display="inline" alttext="3.5 micrometers"><mrow><mn>3.5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span>, 200A <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i45" display="inline" alttext="4.6 times 50 millimeters"><mrow><mn>4.6</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>50</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mm</mi></mrow></math></span>; MilliporeSigma) for positive mode, and an Amide ethylene bridged hybrid (BEH) HILIC column (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i46" display="inline" alttext="3.5 micrometers"><mrow><mn>3.5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span>, 130A <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i47" display="inline" alttext="3 times 50 millimeters"><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>×</mo><mn>50</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mm</mi></mrow></math></span>; Waters) in negative mode. Measurement of mass spectral data was performed for a mass-to-charge ratio (m/z) scan range of 85 to 1,275, and the resolution was 120,000 full width at half maximum (FWHM). Additional details on this method are provided by Goodrich et al.<sup><a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup></p>
<p>Following analysis of all samples from the SOLAR and CHS cohorts, mass spectral peaks for metabolites were extracted with apLCMS and xMSanalyzer.<sup><a href="#c63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c63">63</a>,<a href="#c64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c64">64</a></sup> Extraction was performed across all study samples concurrently and was performed separately for each of the four LC-HRMS modes. After extracting LC-MS features, adjustment for inter- and intra-batch variation was performed using a random forest signal correction algorithm based on quality control samples run in tandem with study samples.<sup><a href="#c65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c65">65</a></sup> As part of the random forest signal correction algorithm, features that were not detected in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i48" display="inline" alttext="more than 25 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>25</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span> of samples were removed from further analysis. Features were also removed from further analysis if the coefficient of variability in all quality control samples post-correction was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i49" display="inline" alttext="more than 30 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>30</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>. After LC-MS data processing, the total number of features included in data analysis was 23,166, including 3,711 features from the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i50" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 18 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>18</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>-negative mode, 5,069 features from the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i51" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 18 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>18</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>-positive mode, 7,442 features from the HILIC-negative mode, and 6,944 features from the HILIC-positive mode.</p></section><section id="s2.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical Analysis</h3>
<p>Differences in PFAS levels and participant characteristics between cohorts were examined using independent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i52" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic t"><mi>t</mi></math></span>-tests (for natural log-transformed PFAS concentrations and continuous participant characteristics) and chi-square tests (for categorical participant characteristics). Spearman correlation coefficients were calculated to examine associations between PFAS levels within each cohort. All analyses were performed with R (version 4.1.2; R Development Core Team).</p></section><section id="s2.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolome-Wide Association Study</h3>
<p>To examine associations between PFAS mixtures and each metabolite, we performed a metabolome-wide association study (MWAS) using a Bayesian hierarchical regression modeling approach with g-computation (BHRM-g). We implemented a Bayesian g-computation approach<sup><a href="#c66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c66">66</a>,<a href="#c67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c67">67</a></sup> to obtain both individual PFAS-specific estimates conditional on all other PFAS in the model and a single mixture effect estimate for the overall PFAS mixture. The approach is similar to quantile g-computation.<sup><a href="#c68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c68">68</a></sup> Specifically, BHRM-g combines: <em>a</em>) a g-prior specification for the corresponding exposure effects to provide robust estimation of highly correlated exposures,<sup><a href="#c69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c69">69</a></sup>
<em>b</em>) a Bayesian stochastic selection procedure to estimate the posterior inclusion probability (PIP) of each PFAS in the PFAS mixtures,<sup><a href="#c67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c67">67</a></sup> and <em>c</em>) Bayesian g-computation in a potential outcome framework for estimating the overall mixture effect based on two hypothetical exposure profiles (explained in further detail below).<sup><a href="#c67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c67">67</a></sup> In contrast to Bayesian Kernel Machine Regression, which uses Gaussian process regression and models a nonlinear dose–response relationship, BHRM-g estimates a mixture effect from the additive terms from a regression model with each exposure and forces explicit specification of nonlinear effects.<sup><a href="#c70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c70">70</a></sup> We modeled the dose–response with a linear function that allows for the calculation of a single monotonic effect estimate that is not dependent on the baseline exposure profile; this aids in interpretation and allows for additional downstream analysis. In this analysis, we independently fit the following model for each metabolite <em>Y</em>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="e1"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="m1" display="block" alttext="uppercase italic y begin subscript lowercase italic i end subscript equals lowercase alpha begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript plus summation from uppercase italic p of lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript lowercase beta begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript uppercase italic x begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript plus summation from uppercase italic q of lowercase delta begin subscript lowercase italic q end subscript uppercase italic u begin subscript lowercase italic q end subscript plus lowercase epsilon begin subscript lowercase italic i end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mi>P</mi></munder><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mi>Q</mi></munder><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">δ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i53" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic x begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is a scaled variable for PFAS exposure <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M1" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></math></span> with corresponding estimate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i54" display="inline" alttext="lowercase beta begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i55" display="inline" alttext="lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is a binary variable indicating the inclusion of PFAS exposure <em>p</em> in the mixture; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i56" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic u begin subscript lowercase italic q end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is a set of <em>q</em> covariates with corresponding effect estimates <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i57" display="inline" alttext="lowercase delta begin subscript lowercase italic q end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">δ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<p>To obtain robust estimates in the presence of highly correlated exposures, we included a second-stage g-prior on the effect estimates of the form <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i58" display="inline" alttext="lowercase beta approximately uppercase italic n begin subscript uppercase italic p end subscript open parenthesis 0, lowercase g lowercase sigma from superscript 2 to subscript uppercase italic y open parenthesis uppercase italic x prime uppercase italic x close parenthesis begin superscript negative 1 end superscript close parenthesis"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi><mo>∼</mo><msub><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi>g</mi><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msubsup><msup><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo>′</mo><mi>X</mi></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>.<sup><a href="#c71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c71">71</a>–<a href="#c74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c74">74</a></sup> Here, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i59" display="inline" alttext="lowercase sigma from superscript 2 to subscript uppercase italic y"><mrow><msubsup><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> is the variance of the outcome; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i60" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic g"><mi>g</mi></math></span> is a scalar with a specified hyper-g prior<sup><a href="#c72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c72">72</a>,<a href="#c75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c75">75</a>,<a href="#c76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c76">76</a></sup>; and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i61" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic x prime uppercase italic x"><mrow><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>′</mo></mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow></math></span> is the covariance matrix.<sup><a href="#c77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c77">77</a></sup> In this model, the scalar <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i62" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic g"><mi>g</mi></math></span> controls the shrinkage toward the prior mean of zero and the dispersion of the posterior covariance via a shrinkage factor of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i63" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic g divided by open parenthesis 1 plus lowercase italic g close parenthesis"><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mo>+</mo><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi>g</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. Similar to regularized regression approaches, the resulting posterior estimate can be expressed as a function of this shrinkage <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i64" display="inline" alttext="lowercase beta tilde equals open bracket lowercase italic g divided by open parenthesis 1 plus lowercase italic g close parenthesis close bracket lowercase beta hat"><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mo>[</mo><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mo>+</mo><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi>g</mi></mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>]</mo></mrow></mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi><mo>^</mo></mover></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i65" display="inline" alttext="lowercase beta hat"><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi><mo>^</mo></mover></math></span> is the maximum likelihood estimate.</p>
<p>To facilitate model selection, we included a binary variable <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i66" display="inline" alttext="lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript equals open brace 0,1 closed brace"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>{</mo><mn>0,1</mn><mo>}</mo></mrow></math></span> in the first-stage linear predictor component of the model, indicating the inclusion of each PFAS. We included a beta-binomial prior for model selection such that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i67" display="inline" alttext="lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript approximately Bernoulli open parenthesis lowercase pi close parenthesis"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mtext> </mtext><mo>∼</mo><mi>B</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">π</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>.<sup><a href="#c78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c78">78</a></sup> Within this framework, the PIP on the individual <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i68" display="inline" alttext="lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase italic p end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is the posterior probability that the coefficient is nonzero. Inference for the Bayesian model was achieved with Markov chain Monte Carlo techniques using JAGs and the R package rjags.<sup><a href="#c79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c79">79</a>,<a href="#c80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c80">80</a></sup> The model was initialized in an adaptive mode with 4,000 iterations to increase the efficiency, and we used Markov Chain for a burn-in period with 1,000 iterations. To generate the posterior distribution of the parameters, we updated the model with 5,000 iterations.</p>
<p>Finally, we used g-computation to yield a single effect estimate (i.e., a mixture effect) that captures the impact of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i69" display="inline" alttext="one standard deviation"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mtext>-standard deviation</mtext><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mo>(</mo><mtext>SD</mtext><mo>)</mo></mrow></math></span> increase in levels of all exposures simultaneously.<sup><a href="#c66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c66">66</a>–<a href="#c68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c68">68</a></sup> This is performed by estimating the difference in the outcome between two hypothetical exposure profiles based on the distribution of PFAS in the study samples. In this study, the low exposure profile is defined as setting each log-transformed and standardized PFAS at a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i70" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic z"><mi>Z</mi></math></span>-score of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i71" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.5"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span> (i.e., the 30.8 percentile) and the high exposure profile is defined as setting each log-transformed and standardized PFAS at a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i72" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic z"><mi>Z</mi></math></span>-score of 0.5 (i.e., the 69.1 percentile). Specifically, we used posterior predictive distributions to estimate a single mixture risk difference (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i73" display="inline" alttext="uppercase psi begin subscript risk difference end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>D</mi></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>), such that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i74" display="inline" alttext="uppercase psi begin subscript risk difference end subscript equals uppercase psi begin subscript lowercase italic x asterisk equals 0.5 end subscript minus uppercase psi begin subscript lowercase italic x asterisk equals negative 0.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>D</mi></mrow></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi><mrow><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi><mrow><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>*</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i75" display="inline" alttext="uppercase psi begin subscript lowercase italic x asterisk end subscript equals summation from uppercase italic p of lowercase gamma begin subscript lowercase p end subscript lowercase beta begin subscript lowercase p end subscript lowercase italic x asterisk begin subscript lowercase p end subscript"><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi><mrow><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>*</mo></msup></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mo>∑</mo><mi>P</mi></munder><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">β</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msubsup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>*</mo></mrow></msubsup></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i76" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic x asterisk"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>*</mo></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> is the counterfactual profile for the log-transformed and standardized exposures with all exposures set to “low” (30.8% levels with an <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i77" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic x asterisk equals negative 0.5"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>*</mo></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span>) and to “high” (69.1% levels with an <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i78" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic x asterisk equals 0.5"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>*</mo></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow></math></span>). The PFAS concentrations for the low and high exposure profiles in the SOLAR and CHS cohorts are provided in Table S1.</p>
<p>Effect estimates from the BHRM-g models are reported as the posterior mean and 95% Bayesian credible intervals (BCIs). Associations between the PFAS mixture and individual metabolites were selected for further analysis based on a 95% BCI not containing zero. Inputs for the pathway enrichment analysis used the PFAS mixture effect (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i79" display="inline" alttext="uppercase psi begin subscript risk difference end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>D</mi></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>), dividing the posterior mean by the posterior variance to obtain a Wald test statistic and corresponding <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i80" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-value. False discovery rate (FDR)–adjusted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i81" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-values were calculated with the Benjamini–Hochberg method to account for multiple comparisons.<sup><a href="#c81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c81">81</a></sup> Adjusted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i82" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-values were calculated within each cohort separately and are represented in the text as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i83" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic q"><mi>q</mi></math></span>-values. Evidence of a significant association between the PFAS mixture and individual metabolites was defined as a 95% BCI for the PFAS mixture effect not containing zero. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i84" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic q less than 0.05"><mrow><mi>q</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow></math></span> was considered as additional evidence of a significant association.</p>
<p>Prior to the MWAS, raw intensity values of LC-MS features were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i85" display="inline" alttext="log base 2 transformed"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mtext> transformed</mtext></mrow></math></span> and normalized to a standard normal distribution to satisfy regression assumptions and obtain comparable effect estimates across all metabolites. Because metabolite intensity was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i86" display="inline" alttext="log base 2 transformed"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mtext> transformed</mtext></mrow></math></span> and scaled prior to analysis, the effect estimate (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i87" display="inline" alttext="uppercase psi begin subscript risk difference end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ψ</mi></mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>D</mi></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) is also equivalent to a standardized mean difference calculated between a hypothetical group of individuals with all PFAS at the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i88" display="inline" alttext="approximately 70"><mrow><mo>∼</mo><mn>70</mn></mrow></math></span>th percentile vs. a hypothetical group of individuals with all PFAS at the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i89" display="inline" alttext="approximately 30"><mrow><mo>∼</mo><mn>30</mn></mrow></math></span>th percentile.</p>
<p>Finally, for BHRM-g to provide a mixture effect estimate interpretable as the change in the outcome when increasing all PFAS in the mixture by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i90" display="inline" alttext="one standard deviation"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mtext> SD</mtext></mrow></math></span>, all exposures in the mixture must be scaled to a mean of zero and a standard deviation of one. In small sample sizes, extremely positively skewed exposures can also cause instability, and in our data, several PFAS, including PFHxS and PFNA, were highly positively skewed. Therefore, before analysis, PFAS were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i91" display="inline" alttext="log base 2 transformed"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">log</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mtext> transformed</mtext></mrow></math></span> and scaled to a mean of zero and standard deviation of one. The R code for performing a single BHRM-g regression is provided in Supplemental Material, “Supplemental Code.” The R code for all analyses performed in this study is available on GitHub at <a href="https://github.com/chatzilab/PFAS_metabolomics_EHP_2022" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://github.com/chatzilab/PFAS_metabolomics_EHP_2022</a>.</p></section><section id="s2.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolite Annotation and Pathway Enrichment</h3>
<p>Following the BHRM-g MWAS, we used the PFAS mixture effect estimate to perform a pathway enrichment analysis using the MS peaks to pathways module from MetaboAnalyst (version 5.0).<sup><a href="#c82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c82">82</a></sup> We used version 2 of the MS peaks to pathways module, which accounts for retention time for more accurate metabolite annotation.<sup><a href="#c83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c83">83</a></sup> For this analysis, we included LC-MS features from both positive and negative ionization. We used a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i92" display="inline" alttext="5.0 parts per million"><mrow><mn>5.0</mn><mtext>-ppm</mtext></mrow></math></span> mass tolerance, a 0.05 <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i93" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-value threshold, and the Human reference pathways of the MetaFishNet (MFN) database.<sup><a href="#c84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c84">84</a></sup> We used the integrated metabolic pathway enrichment analysis,<sup><a href="#c85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c85">85</a></sup> which determines pathway enrichment using an overrepresentation analysis<sup><a href="#c86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c86">86</a></sup> and a Gene Set Enrichment Analysis.<sup><a href="#c87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c87">87</a></sup> Significance of metabolic pathways across cohorts was determined by combining <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i94" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-values using a weighted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i95" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic z"><mi>Z</mi></math></span>-test.<sup><a href="#c88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c88">88</a></sup> For the pathway enrichment analysis, statistical significance was based on a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i96" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-value threshold of 0.05. Metabolic pathways identified as significantly enriched were included in follow-up analysis if they included at least four significant metabolites in either the SOLAR or CHS cohorts.</p></section><section id="s2.8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Sensitivity Analysis</h3>
<p>Due to differences in the SOLAR and CHS cohorts, including differences in developmental stages and exposure profiles, the primary method for combining results across the two cohorts in this study was to perform a meta-analysis on the effect estimates obtained from study-specific regression models with study-specific covariates. This method avoids many of the potential pitfalls of pooled analysis.<sup><a href="#c89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c89">89</a></sup> However, to assess the impact of this analytic choice, we conducted a sensitivity analysis by performing the entire analytic workflow using pooled data from the two cohorts. Because of differences in covariates between cohorts, a minimal set of covariates was used for this analysis, including sex, developmental stage (based on Tanner stage), parental education, and study wave/cohort. Given that Tanner stage was not measured in the CHS (young adult) cohort, Tanner stage was imputed as stage 5 for all individuals from this cohort. For the pooled analysis, study wave/cohort included three levels (Wave 1 SOLAR, Wave 2 SOLAR, and CHS) and was included in the model as two numeric dummy variables. For the pooled analysis, in addition to examining metabolites associated with significantly enriched metabolic pathways, we also further examined associations of the PFAS mixtures with individual metabolites associated with any of the significantly enriched metabolic pathways from the individual cohort analysis.</p></section></section><section id="s3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="s3.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Characteristics of the Study Population</h3>
<p>Participant characteristics for the SOLAR and CHS cohorts are presented in <a href="#t1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, and plasma PFAS concentrations for the SOLAR and CHS cohorts are provided in <a href="#t2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>. In both cohorts, PFOS, PFHxS, PFHpS, PFOA, and PFNA were detected in all participants. PFDA was detected in 99.4% of participants from the SOLAR cohort and 98.5% of participants from the CHS cohort. PFAS levels ranged from uncorrelated to strongly positively correlated, with Spearman correlation coefficients between 0.01 and 0.93 in the SOLAR cohort and between 0.10 and 0.93 in the CHS cohort (Figure S2). Concentrations of all PFAS were significantly higher in the SOLAR cohort compared with the CHS cohort (<a href="#t2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). In both cohorts, PFAS concentrations were similar to those reported in the appropriate time period and age-matched NHANES (Table S2).<sup><a href="#c60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c60">60</a></sup></p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="t1"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
